`` OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s
Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2025
Executive Summary
The Veterans Access, Choice, and Accountability Act of 2014 and VA Choice and Quality
Employment Act of 2017 requires the VA Office of Inspector General (OIG) to determine,
annually, a minimum of five clinical and five nonclinical Veterans Health Administration (VHA)
occupations with the largest staffing shortages within each VHA medical center (facility).1
Pursuant to this requirement, the OIG conducted a review to identify those severe staffing
shortages by occupation. The OIG also compared the number of severe occupational staffing
shortages against the previous seven years’ reports to assess changes.
This is the 12th OIG report in a series on occupational staffing shortages and the 8th to identify
severe occupational staffing shortages at the facility level. The OIG deployed a questionnaire to
VHA-identified facility points of contact to determine severe occupational staffing shortages at
each facility.2 It should be noted that the OIG does not verify or otherwise confirm the
questionnaire responses. Among the most significant findings in this year’s staffing report were
the following:
· In fiscal year (FY) 2025, VHA facilities reported a total of 4,434 severe
occupational staffing shortages, a 50 percent increase from FY 2024 in which
facilities reported 2,959 total shortage occupations.3
· Ninety-four percent of facilities reported severe occupational staffing shortages for
Medical Officer occupations, and 79 percent of facilities reported severe shortages
for Nurse occupations.4
1Veterans Access, Choice, and Accountability Act of 2014, Pub. L. No. 113-146, 128 Stat. 1754 (2014); VA Choice
and Quality Employment Act of 2017, Pub. L. No. 115-46, 131 Stat. 958 (2017) § 201.
2The OIG did not request information indicating the rationale for facilities’ identification of occupational staffing
shortages. The OIG did not assess possible impacts of workforce reshaping efforts initiated prior to the deployment
of the questionnaire such as the Deferred Resignation Program. On January 28, 2025, OPM announced the Deferred
Resignation Program, which provided eligible employees an option to submit a deferred resignation and be
exempted from return-to-office requirements while retaining pay and benefits until September 30, 2025. “Deferred
Resignation Email to Federal Employees” OPM, accessed June 11, 2025, https://www.opm.gov/fork/original-email-
to-employees.
3A severe shortage refers to particular occupations that are difficult to fill, and a shortage exists when criteria set
forth in 5 Code of Federal Regulations (C.F.R.) § 337.204, Severe Shortage of Candidates, are applied, and not
necessarily an indication of vacancies. Vacancy refers to a specific unoccupied position and is distinct from the
designation of a severe shortage. For example, a facility could identify an occupation as a severe occupational
shortage, which could have no vacant positions or 100 vacant positions.
4Medical Officer and Nurse were further analyzed using VHA assignment codes that designate specialties within
the corresponding Office of Personnel Management occupational series. Assessing severe shortages at the level of
these specialty assignment codes instead of the broader occupational series codes allowed the OIG to assess severe
shortages for the Medical Officer and Nurse series at a more detailed level.

frequently reported severe nonclinical occupational staffing shortage and the most
frequently reported of all occupations.

The OIG made no recommendations, but encourages VA leaders to use these review results to
inform staffing initiatives and organizational changes.

`` OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s
Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2025
Introduction
The purpose of this review is for the VA Office of Inspector General (OIG) to report a minimum
of five clinical and five nonclinical Veterans Health Administration (VHA) occupations that
have the largest staffing shortages at each VHA medical center (facility) as required by the
Veterans Access, Choice, and Accountability Act (VACAA) of 2014 and VA Choice and
Quality Employment Act (VCQEA) of 2017.1 The OIG also compared the number of severe
occupational staffing shortages in fiscal year (FY) 2025 against those the OIG identified in the
FY 2018 through FY 2024 reports.2 This is the 12th iteration in a series of annual OIG reports
determining occupational staffing shortages in VHA and the 8th to identify severe occupational
staffing shortages at the facility level.3
Background
Veterans Access, Choice, and Accountability Act and VA Choice
and Quality Employment Act
In May 2014, the OIG reported ongoing concerns regarding access to VHA care, scheduling
practices, and excessive wait times at the VA Phoenix Health Care System.4 Congress passed
VACAA in response to these concerns. VACAA required the OIG to determine, annually, the
five VHA occupations with the largest staffing shortages over the previous five-year period.
After the passage of the VCQEA, the scope of the OIG’s work expanded to include an annual

2The fiscal year for the federal government is a 12-month period from October 1 through September 30 and is

Occupational Series and Assignment Codes
The principle of “providing equal pay for substantially equal work” was established by the
Classification Act of 1949.5 The Office of Personnel Management (OPM), after consulting with
federal agencies, created occupational series to classify positions with similar work
specializations and qualifications, for instance the 0602–Medical Officer series.
VHA further indicates Medical Officer and Nurse specialties using assignment codes within the
corresponding OPM occupational series. For example, an Infection Control Nurse would have an
OPM occupational series of 0610–Nurse, and a VHA assignment code of 74. VHA classifies
occupations as clinical or nonclinical beyond the hiring authority (Title 38, Hybrid Title 38, and
Title 5).6 VHA has labeled Infection Control Nurse a clinical occupation, and nurses are
appointed under Title 38.
Hybrid Title 38 Hiring in VHA
VACAA and VCQEA established authority for the VA Secretary to directly recruit and appoint
qualified personnel to occupations determined to have the largest staffing shortages by the OIG.
For FY 2025, VHA sought and received the authority for a waiver of veterans’ preference
requirements for external applicants to Hybrid Title 38 occupations from the VA Secretary for 13
of these occupations, as informed by OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s
Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages, Fiscal Year 2024 and VHA Workforce Management
and Consulting’s 2024 VHA Shortage Occupation Report.7 See appendix A for the list of VHA
5 Classification Act of 1949, Pub. L. No. 81-429, 63 Stat. 954 (1949).
6 VHA National Workforce Planning, Workforce Management and Consulting, Workforce Solutions, FY 2024 VHA
Workforce and Succession Strategic Plan for FY 2025-2028. VHA defines clinical occupations to “include
personnel who provide direct patient care or services incident to patient care and whose efforts have a therapeutic
intent (e.g., physical, mental, social and spiritual).” VHA defines nonclinical occupations as “administrative and
maintenance personnel who do not provide direct patient care or services incident to patient care.”
7 The VA Secretary may “recruit and appoint qualified individuals into occupations reported as having the largest
staffing shortages” by the OIG notwithstanding competitive service and Veterans’ preference eligibility

(OCHCO) Bulletin, “Annual Determination of Staffing Shortages,” October 1, 2024. The aforementioned Office of
the Chief Human Capital Officer Bulletin identified 13 shortage occupations for which veterans’ preference
eligibility requirements were waived. The waiver of veterans’ preference for these 13 occupations is in effect
through September 30, 2025. A list of these occupations can be found in appendix A; VA OIG, OIG Determination
of Veterans Health Administration’s Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages Fiscal Year 2024; VHA Workforce
Management and Consulting, 2024 VHA Shortage Occupation Report, accessed May 15, 2025,
https://dvagov.sharepoint.com/sites/vhaforce/SitePages/WFPReports.aspx. (This site is not publicly accessible.)

Scope and Methodology
Staffing Questionnaire Development and Distribution
The OIG deployed a questionnaire to VHA-identified facility points of contact to identify severe
occupational staffing shortages at each facility across VHA. To facilitate identification of severe
occupational staffing shortages, the OIG provided facility points of contact with the definition
for a severe shortage of candidates in 5 Code of Federal Regulations (C.F.R.) Section (§)
337.204 (2025).9
The OIG requested, and VHA provided, the points of contact who would be completing the
questionnaire on behalf of each facility. The OIG’s questionnaire listed OPM occupational series
used by VHA and VHA assignment codes for Medical Officer and Nurse specialties.10
8 Underlined terms are hyperlinks to another section of the report. To return to point of origin, press and hold the
“alt” and “left arrow” keys together.
9Severe Shortage of Candidates, 5 C.F.R. § 337.204 (2025), states
(a) OPM will determine when a severe shortage of candidates exists for particular occupations, grades (or
equivalent), and/or geographic locations. OPM may decide independently that such a shortage exists, or may
make this decision in response to a written request from an agency.
(b) An agency when requesting direct-hire authority under this section, or OPM when deciding independently,
must identify the position or positions that are difficult to fill and must provide supporting evidence that
demonstrates the existence of a severe shortage of candidates with respect to the position(s). The evidence
should include, as applicable, information about:
(1) The results of workforce planning and analysis;
(2) Employment trends including the local or national labor market;
(3) The existence of nationwide or geographic skills shortages;
(4) Agency efforts, including recruitment initiatives, use of other appointing authorities (e.g., schedule A,
schedule B) and flexibilities, training and development programs tailored to the position(s), and an explanation
of why these recruitment and training efforts have not been sufficient;
(5) The availability and quality of candidates;
(6) The desirability of the geographic location of the position(s);
(7) The desirability of the duties and/or work environment associated with the position(s); and
(8) Other pertinent information such as selective placement factors or other special requirements of the position,
as well as agency use of hiring flexibilities such as recruitment or retention allowances or special salary rates.
10 The list of 593 occupations included those provided by VHA’s Office of Human Capital Management. VHA
reported that some occupations included in the questionnaire were consolidated at the VISN level, national level, or
may have been employed at both the facility and VISN levels.

38 U.S. Code § 7412(a) requires the OIG to determine the top five clinical and top five
nonclinical occupations with severe shortages at each facility.11 Notably, VHA reported that
some occupations previously hired at the facility level were realigned to the Veterans Integrated
Service Network (VISN) level, such as Human Resources Management. This realignment may
have caused certain occupations that once appeared within the top facility-identified
occupational shortages to no longer appear. See appendix B for a complete list of OPM
occupational series and VHA assignment codes included in the survey. Facility points of contact
were asked to indicate those occupations considered to have a severe occupational staffing
shortage and also had the option to report that a facility had no severe occupational staffing
shortages.12
The OIG distributed a link to the web-based questionnaire via email to VHA-identified facility
points of contact beginning on March 26, 2025, with a required completion date of
April 9, 2025.13 The OIG confirmed receipt of completed surveys from all 139 facilities on
April 24, 2025.
The OIG did not independently verify VHA data for accuracy or completeness.
Changes in Severe Occupational Shortages Questionnaire
The OIG compared FY 2025 to FY 2018–2024 questionnaire responses to evaluate changes in
severe occupational staffing shortages across VHA. Some limitations that may affect
comparisons include changes in position categorization, veteran demand for healthcare services,
or alterations to the questionnaire. Through refinement of position categorization, the total
number of occupations provided to the OIG has changed to reflect the most current occupational
codes. An increase or decrease in the number of occupations alters the total possible number of
occupations a facility can identify as having severe shortages. Changes in veteran demand affect
facility staffing needs, which, in turn, may change how facilities identify staffing shortages.
Changes to the questionnaire may also affect how facilities identify shortages. Questionnaires

from FYs 2018–2020 were distributed via email using Microsoft Excel, whereas FY 2021–2025
questionnaires were collected via a web-based questionnaire using Microsoft InfoPath, and via
Microsoft Forms in 2025. This resulted in differences in how the questionnaire looked to
respondents. Additionally, FY 2021–2025 respondents were VHA-identified facility points of
contact instead of facility directors, and the OIG did not ask for information such as reasons for
shortages or for shortages to be ranked. Although the OIG requested facilities to identify severe
occupational staffing shortages across FYs 2018–2025, and there were no changes in how
shortages were defined, the potential for bias may exist stemming from the aforementioned
changes.
Questionnaire Analysis
The OIG identified an occupation as a severe occupational staffing shortage when it was
designated as such by the VHA-identified facility point of contact. The OIG counted the total
number of times the occupation was identified as a severe occupational staffing shortage among
all facilities to determine shortages across VHA.
Facility points of contact could identify severe shortages for Medical Officer and Nurse
occupations at the OPM occupational series level, the VHA assignment code level, or both. The
OIG only included individual Medical Officer and Nurse assignment code occupations in the
reported list of top severe occupational staffing shortages. Separately, the OIG derived the
frequency of severe shortages for Medical Officer and Nurse if the facility points of contact
indicated either the OPM occupational series or any of the related VHA assignment codes as
shortages.
Authorities
In the absence of current VA or VHA policy, the OIG considered previous guidance to be in
effect until superseded by an updated or recertified directive, handbook, or other policy
document on the same or similar issue(s).
Oversight authority to review the programs and operations of VA medical facilities is authorized
by the Inspector General Act of 1978, as amended, 5 U.S.C. §§ 401–424. The OIG reviews
available evidence within a specified scope and methodology and makes recommendations to
VA leaders, if warranted. Findings and recommendations do not define a standard of care or
establish legal liability.
The OIG conducted the review in accordance with Quality Standards for Inspection and
Evaluation published by the Council of the Inspectors General on Integrity and Efficiency.

OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2025
Review Results
The OIG deployed questionnaires to 139 VHA facilities to determine the occupations designated
as severe shortages.14 To determine the magnitude of severe occupational staffing shortages at
VHA, the OIG counted the total number of times an occupation was identified as a shortage.The
OIG also compared FYs 2018–2025 questionnaire responses to evaluate changes in severe
occupational staffing shortages across VHA.
Total Number of Severe Occupational Shortages
In FY 2025, VHA facilities reported a total of 4,434 severe occupational staffing shortages, a
50 percent increase from FY 2024 in which facilities reported 2,959 total shortage occupations.
The OIG also compared severe occupational shortages at the facility level. All 139 facilities
reported staffing shortages. The number of facilities’ severe occupational staffing shortage
designations ranged from 2 to 153, including both clinical and nonclinical occupations.15 The
greatest number of clinical occupational shortages reported by an individual facility was 116, and
the greatest number of nonclinical occupational shortages was 52.16
Medical Officer and Nurse Occupational Staffing Shortages
Because VHA utilizes assignment codes to designate specialties within the corresponding OPM
occupational series, Medical Officer and Nurse occupations were analyzed separately from the
other occupations. The OIG derived the frequency of Medical Officer and Nurse severe
occupational staffing shortages if the facility indicated either the OPM occupational series or any
of its related VHA assignment codes as shortages. For FY 2025, the derived frequency for
Medical Officer shortages was 94 percent (131 of 139 facilities). The derived frequency for
Nurse shortages was 79 percent (110 of 139 facilities).
Every year since 2014, the Medical Officer and Nurse occupations were identified as severe
shortages in OIG’s annual determination of occupational staffing shortages reports. VHA is able
to noncompetitively appoint individuals to both of these Title 38 occupations in accordance with
VA guidance.17
14For purposes of this report, the OIG used the term occupation when referencing either OPM occupational series or
VHA assignment codes.
15VA Long Beach Healthcare System in California (VISN 22, Station 600) reported the most severe occupational
staffing shortages among all facilities, with 153 reported shortages. This was the greatest number of shortage
occupations reported by a single facility across FYs 2018–2025.
16VA Long Beach Healthcare System in California (VISN 22, Station 600) designated the most clinical severe
occupational staffing shortages. Minneapolis VA Health Care System in Minnesota (VISN 23, Station 618)
designated the most nonclinical severe occupational staffing shortages.

Top Occupational Shortages
In FY 2025, 41 occupations were identified as severe staffing shortages by at least 20 percent of
VHA facilities, excluding the Medical Officer and Nurse OPM occupational series (see appendix
Efor the list of occupations reported by at least 20 percent of VHA facilities).18 This represents
the highest number of occupations reported by at least 20 percent of facilities across FYs 2018-
2025 (see figure 2).
18The OIG excludes the Medical Officer and Nurse OPM occupational series level occupations in this analysis
because VHA utilizes assignment codes to designate specialties within the corresponding OPM occupational series.
Assessing severe shortages at the assignment code level instead of the broader occupational series codes enabled the
OIG to assess severe shortages for the Medical Officer and Nurse series in more detail. The criterion of at least 20
percent of VHA facilities designating an occupation as a severe occupational staffing shortage is consistent with
prior OIG reports on VHA occupational staffing shortages as well as the criterion for a national shortage of
occupations utilized by Workforce Management and Consulting in their annual shortage occupation report.
However, there is no requirement for the OIG to use this criterion as a threshold for determining severe occupational
staffing shortages at VHA. VHA Workforce Management and Consulting, 2024 VHA Shortage Occupation Report.

Top Five Clinical Shortage Occupations
Psychology was the most frequently reported clinical severe occupational staffing shortage in FY
2025 and also the most frequently reported Hybrid Title 38 severe shortage occupation, with 57
percent of facilities (79 of 139) reporting it as a shortage. This was a decrease from FY 2024,
when 61 percent of facilities (85 of 139) reported Psychology as a shortage. Psychology has been
in the top five reported clinical severe occupational staffing shortages since FY 2019 and was
among the top five most frequently reported shortages in FY 2018, when VHA did not have a
formal designation for clinical and nonclinical occupations.
Of the top five most reported clinical severe occupational staffing shortages in FY 2025, all but
Nursing Assistant have been reported by at least 20 percent of facilities since FY 2018. Nursing
Assistant was first reported by at least 20 percent of facilities in FY 2021 and has been reported
by at least 20 percent since (see figure 3).

*Occupations within the Medical Officer occupational series.
Top Five Nonclinical Shortage Occupations
In FY 2025, the OIG found that 58 percent of facilities (80 of 139) designated Police as a severe
occupational staffing shortage, making it the most frequently reported nonclinical shortage
occupation and most frequently reported of all occupations. The Police occupation was among
the top five most frequently reported nonclinical shortages in each year since 2019, when VHA
first made official designations for clinical and nonclinical occupations, and the seventh most
frequently reported shortage among all occupations in 2018. The Custodial Worker occupation
was reported by 57 percent of facilities (79 of 139), making it the second most reported shortage
occupation. Of the five most frequently reported nonclinical shortage occupations, only the
Supply Clerical and Technician occupation has never been among the top five.

Conclusion
In FY 2025, VHA facilities reported a total of 4,434 severe occupational staffing shortages, a
50 percent increase from FY 2024 across VHA. The number of facilities’ severe occupational
staffing shortage designations ranged from 2 to 153, including both clinical and nonclinical
occupations. These data points show that a wide range of severe occupational staffing shortages
exist when comparing shortages at the facility level.
Every year since 2014, the Medical Officer and Nurse occupations were identified as severe
shortages in OIG’s annual determination of occupational staffing shortages reports. VHA is able
to noncompetitively appoint individuals to both of these Title 38 occupations in accordance with
VA guidance. The OIG determined that 94 percent of facilities had severe shortages for Medical
Officer, and 79 percent of facilities had severe shortages for Nurse. Despite the ability to make
noncompetitive appointments for such occupations, VHA continues to experience severe
occupational staffing shortages for these occupations that are fundamental to the delivery of
health care.
Forty-one occupations (excluding Medical Officer and Nurse) were identified as severe
occupational staffing shortages by at least 20 percent of facilities. This represents the highest
VA OIG 25-01135-196 | Page 10 | August 12, 2025

=== PAGE 18 ===
OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2025
number of occupations reported by at least 20 percent of facilities across FYs 2018–2025.
Fifty-seven percent of facilities noted severe occupational staffing shortages for Psychology,
making it the most frequently reported clinical occupation; and 58 percent of facilities noted
severe occupational staffing shortages for Police, making it the most frequently reported
nonclinical occupation.
This report is a descriptive review and is intended for informational purposes. The OIG did not
make recommendations but emphasizes the importance of VHA’s continued assessment of
severe occupational staffing shortages given the increase in shortages reported from FY 2024 to
FY 2025. Additionally, the OIG encourages VHA’s efforts to complete work on open
recommendations from the August 2021 OIG Report, Review of Veterans Health Administration
Staffing Models.19
Due to the timing of the FY 2025 OIG staffing shortage questionnaire, the reported impact on
staffing from OPM’s Deferred Resignation Program and VA’s workforce reshaping efforts are
not fully reflected in this report.
19VA OIG, Review of Veterans Health Administration Staffing Models, Report No. 20-01508-214, August 19, 2021.

=== PAGE 302 ===
OIG Determination of Veterans Health Administration’s Severe Occupational Staffing Shortages
Fiscal Year 2025
Appendix E: Frequency of Severe Occupational
Staffing Shortages Designated by at Least 20 Percent
of Facilities
Occupations designated by at least one in five facilities were included in table E.1. As a result,
not all occupations designated as a severe shortage by each facility were included. For example,
the Medical Officer assignment code 16 Emergency Medicine does not appear in the table but
was identified by 27 facilities as a severe occupational staffing shortage. This table provides
insight as to how often an occupation was reported as a severe occupational staffing shortage
throughout VHA and can be used to bring awareness of systemic shortages.
Table 1 should not be considered the sole measure of VHA-wide severe occupational staffing
shortages. It does not address all shortages throughout VHA and does not account for other
dimensions that could be used to determine shortages such as the priority of a severe
occupational staffing shortage at a given facility, or the number of staff on board at the facility.
For example, one facility may consider an occupation as its number 1 shortage, despite having
five staff on board, while another facility may consider that same occupation as its number 30
shortage with only two staff on board.
The OIG did not consider facility priority or number of staff on board when determining the
frequency of occupations reported as severe staffing shortages across VHA. Further, the impact
that filling a shortage might have on a facility cannot be assessed by the questionnaire results.
Severe occupational shortages are not necessarily an indication of vacancies. A severe shortage
refers to particular occupations that are difficult to fill, and a shortage exists when criteria set
forth in 5 C.F.R. § 337.204, Severe Shortage of Candidates, are applied.20 Vacancy refers to an
unoccupied position and is distinct from the designation of a severe shortage. For example, a
facility could identify an occupation as a severe occupational shortage, which could have no
vacant positions or 100 vacant positions.
205 C.F.R. § 337.204.
VA OIG 25-01135-196 | Page 295 | August 12, 2025
